Concentration Properties of Random Codes
Lan V. Truong, Giuseppe Cocco, Josep Font-Segura, Albert Guillén i Fàbregas

Abstract
This paper studies concentration properties of random codes. Specifically, we show that, for discrete memoryless channels, the error exponent of a randomly generated code with pairwise-independent codewords converges in probability to its expectation—the typical error exponent. For high rates, the result is a consequence of the fact that the random-coding error exponent and the sphere-packing error exponent coincide. For low rates, instead, the convergence is based on the fact that the union bound accurately characterizes the probability of error. The paper also zooms into the behavior at asymptotically low rates, and shows that the error exponent converges in distribution to a Gaussian-like distribution. Finally, we present several results on the convergence of the error probability and error exponent for generic ensembles and channels.

\fail

BSC

CC

FSC

I
Introduction

The
DMC
(
DMC
) has been devoted a lot of interest in information theory ever since in
[
1
]
Shannon showed that for
DMC
there exist codes whose probability of error vanishes with the codewords length for rates below the channel capacity. Since then, one of the most active areas of research in Information Theory has been the study of properties of the probability of error. For rates below capacity, Fano
[
2
]
characterized the exponential decay of the error probability defining the error exponent as the negative normalized logarithm of the ensemble-average error probability, i.e., the
RCE
(
RCE
). In
[
3
]
, Gallager derived the
RCE
in a simpler way and introduced the idea of expurgation in order to obtain an improved exponent the at low rates. A lower bound on the error probability in the
DMC
, called sphere-packing bound, was first introduced in
[
4
]
and it was shown to coincide with the
RCE
for rates higher than a certain critical rate. Nakiboğlu in
[
5
]
recently derived sphere-packing bounds for some stationary memoryless channels using Augustin’s method
[
6
]
.

In
[
7
]
, Barg and Forney studied the random-coding ensemble over the
BSC
(
BSC
) with maximum likelihood decoding and showed that the error exponent of most random codes in the i.i.d. ensemble is close to the so-called
TRC
(
TRC
) exponent, strictly larger than the
RCE
at low rates. Upper and lower bounds on the
TRC
for constant-composition codes and general
DMC
s were provided in
[
8
]
. For the same type of codes and channels, Merhav
[
9
]
determined the exact
TRC
error exponent and a wide class of stochastic decoders called generalized likelihood decoder (GLD), of which maximum-likelihood is a special case. Merhav derived the
TRC
exponent for spherical codes over coloured Gaussian channels
[
10
]
and for random convolutional code ensembles
[
11
]
. The error exponent of a random pairwise-independent constant-composition code with GLD was shown to converge in probability to the TRC in
[
12
]
. The convergence is non-symmetric: the lower tail decays exponentially while the upper tail decays doubly-exponentially. The latter was first established for a limited range of rates in
[
13
]
. The TRC was shown to be universally achievable with the likelihood mutual information decoder in
[
14
]
. For pairwise-independent ensembles and arbitrary channels, Cocco
et al.
showed in
[
15
]
that the probability that the exponent of a given code in the ensemble is smaller than a lower bound on the
TRC
exponent is vanishingly small.

The main motivation of our work is the fact that the aforementioned results highlight the importance of the statistical properties of the error probability and the error exponent across the random-coding ensemble. After describing the main performance metrics of random codes for reliable communication in the next section, namely the error probability and the error exponent, we use the notion of convergence in probability and convergence in distribution to obtain a number of concentration results of such performance metric, seen as sequences of random variables, as the blocklength tends to infinity. Since neither the error probability or the error exponent are sums of i.i.d. terms, our results are based on probability results beyond the central limit theorem, such as the Stein’s method and a novel, modified Wasserstein metric. We anticipate here some of our main results in Sec.
III
, valid for the
DMC
and the i.i.d. and constant-composition ensembles.

•

We show in Theorem
1
that the error exponent converges in probability the
TRC
exponent.

•

In Theorems
2
–
4
we provide bounds on the rate of such convergence.

•

For codes with a constant number of codewords, we obtain in Theorem
5
that the error exponent converges in distribution to a quasi-Gaussian, while for codes with a sub-exponential number of codewords it does converge to a Gaussian, as shown in Theorem
6
.

For general channels under some additional conditions, we obtain in Sec.
IV
the following results.

•

For any channel and capacity-achieving ensemble, Theorem
7
states that the error probability converges to the ensemble average.

•

Theorems
8
–
11
discuss several convergence results relating random properties of the error probability, the random-coding error exponent and the
TRC
exponent.

•

Sufficient conditions for the union bound on the error probability and any general function of the error probability to converge to a Gaussian are respectively described in Theorems
12
–
13
.

The proofs of our main results, including our modified Wasserstein metric, are reported in Sec.
V
.

II
Preliminaries

We consider the problem of transmitting
M_{n}
equiprobable messages over a
DMC
with transition probability
W
and finite input and output alphabets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, respectively. We employ a codebook
\mathscr{c}_{n}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},\cdots,\bm{x}_{M_{n}}\}
with
\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, for
m=1,\ldots,M_{n}
. The conditional distribution of a channel output
\bm{y}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}
given a transmitted codeword
\bm{x}
is given by
W^{n}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}W(y_{i}|x_{i})
.
We consider maximum-likelihood decoding, that is, the decoder produces an estimate of the transmitted codeword as
\hat{\bm{x}}=\argmax_{\bm{x}\in\mathscr{c}_{n}}\,W^{n}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})
. The error probability of such code is

\displaystyle P_{\rm e}(\mathscr{c}_{n})=\frac{1}{M_{n}}\sum_{m=1}^{M_{n}}\mathbb{P}\bigg[\bigcup_{{\bar{m}}\neq m}\{\bm{x}_{m}\to\bm{x}_{\bar{m}}\}\bigg],

(1)

where
\{\bm{x}_{m}\to\bm{x}_{\bar{m}}\}=\{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{Y}:W^{n}(\bm{y}|\bm{x}_{{\bar{m}}})\geq W^{n}(\bm{y}|\bm{x}_{m})\}
is the maximum-likelihood pairwise error event, i.e., the event of deciding in favor of codeword
\bm{x}_{\bar{m}}
when codeword
\bm{x}_{m}
was transmitted.
The error exponent of code
\mathscr{c}_{n}
is defined as

\displaystyle E_{n}(\mathscr{c}_{n})=-\frac{1}{n}\log P_{\mathrm{e}}(\mathscr{c}_{n}).

(2)

Let
R=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log M_{n}
be the rate of the code in bits per channel use. An error exponent
E(R)
is said to be achievable when there exists a sequence of codes
\{\mathscr{c}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}E_{n}(\mathscr{c}_{n})\geq E(R)
. The channel capacity
C
is the supremum of the code rates
R
such that
E(R)>0
.

We next consider the random generation of the codebook. Similarly to random variables,
\mathcal{C}_{n}
denotes a random code, and
\mathscr{c}_{n}
denotes a specific code in the ensemble. In particular, we consider the pairwise-independent random-coding ensemble, i.e., the set of random codes
\mathcal{C}_{n}
whose codewords
\bm{X}_{1},\bm{X}_{2},\cdots,\bm{X}_{M_{n}}
are pairwise-independently generated. We consider the i.i.d. ensemble, in which each codeword is generated according to the distribution

\displaystyle Q^{\rm iid}(\bm{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}Q(x_{i}),

(3)

Q
being the single-letter distribution and the constant-composition ensemble, in which each codeword is generated according to the distribution

\displaystyle Q^{\rm cc}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{T}_{n}(Q_{n})|}\mathds{1}\{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}(Q_{n})\}

(4)

where
\mathcal{T}_{n}(Q_{n})
is the type class of composition
Q_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}(\mathcal{X})
, i.e., all
n
-length sequences whose empirical distribution is
Q_{n}
such that
\max_{x}|Q_{n}(x)-Q(x)|\leq\frac{1}{n}
for a given distribution
Q
.
For a given distribution or composition
Q
, we define the random-coding error exponent
E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
as

\displaystyle E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)=\lim_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbb{E}[P_{\rm e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},Q)],

(5)

where
P_{\rm e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},Q)
denotes the error probability of the random code ensemble
\mathcal{C}_{n}
parametrized by the distribution or composition
Q
and where the expectation is taken over the code ensemble. Eq. (
5
) suggests that
E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
is the asymptotic exponent of the ensemble-average probability of error.
For the i.i.d. ensemble, it is known that
[
3
, Th. 1]

\displaystyle E_{\rm rce}^{\rm iid}(R,Q)=\max_{0\leq\rho\leq 1}\big\{E_{0}^{\rm iid}(\rho,Q)-\rho R\big\}

(6)

with

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\rm iid}(\rho,Q)=-\log\sum_{y}\bigg(\sum_{x}Q(x)W(y|x)^{\frac{1}{1+\rho}}\bigg)^{1+\rho};

(7)

while for the constant-composition ensemble, we have that (
6
) remains valid, but
E_{0}^{\rm iid}(\rho,Q)
is replaced by
[
16
, Eq. (53)]

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\rm cc}(\rho,Q)=\sup_{a(x)}-\log\sum_{y}\biggl(\sum_{x}Q(x)W(y|x)^{\frac{1}{1+\rho}}e^{a(x)-\phi_{a}}\biggr)^{\!\!1+\rho}

(8)

where
a(x)
is an auxiliary function and
\phi_{a}=\sum_{x}Q(x)a(x)
. It is known that for any given
Q
,
E_{\rm rce}^{\rm iid}(R,Q)\leq E_{\rm rce}^{\rm cc}(R,Q)
(see e.g.
[
17
]
).

While
E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
in (
5
) is the limiting exponential rate of decay of the ensemble average probability of error, the typical random-coding exponent
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
is instead defined as the limiting expected error exponent over the ensemble, that is,

\displaystyle E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)=\lim_{n\to\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\log P_{\rm e}(\mathcal{C}_{n},Q)\big].

(9)

We observe that Jensen’s inequality implies that the random-coding error exponent in (
5
) and the typical random-coding error exponent in (
9
) satisfy
E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)\leq E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
. The proofs of our results exploit the idea that there are two rate regimes; one where
E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)<E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
and the other where
E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)=E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
. For DMCs with constant-composition codes
[
9
]
and for i.i.d. codes over the BSC
[
7
]
, the typical error exponent can be further expressed in terms of the expurgated error exponent
E_{\rm ex}(R,Q)
as

\displaystyle E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)=\max\{E_{\rm ex}(2R,Q)+R,E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)\}.

(10)

In the next sections, we derive concentration results of the error probability (
1
) and the error exponent (
2
) of sequences of random codes
{\cal C}_{n}
in the asymptotic regime as
n\to\infty
. As sequence of random variables, we assume throughout the paper that
P_{\rm e}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
and
E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
do not diverge as
n\to\infty
, leaving particular cases such as channels with positive zero-error capacity beyond the scope of this paper. We will use the notion of convergence in probability and convergence in distribution. A sequence of random variables
\{A_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges to
A
in probability, denoted as
A_{n}\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\rm p})}}{{\,\longrightarrow\,}}}A
if for all
\delta>0

[
18
, Sec. 2.2]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[|A_{n}-A|>\delta]=0.

(11)

If
A_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}X_{i}
, where
X_{i},i=1,\dotsc,n
are i.i.d. random variables, then
A=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}]
and (
11
) reduces to the weak law of large numbers
[
18
, Th. 2.2.3]
. The weak law of large numbers is at the core of the asymptotic equipartition property, a widely used tool in information theory to establish the achievable rates using random coding
[
19
]
; it is well known that the asymptotic equipartition property is not sufficient to show the achievability of error exponents. Alternatively to (
11
), we say that a sequence of random variables
\{A_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges to
A
in distribution, denoted as
A_{n}\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\rm d})}}{{\,\longrightarrow\,}}}A
if
[
18
, Sec. 3.2]

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}[A_{n}\leq x]-\mathbb{P}[A\leq x]\big|=0

(12)

for all continuous points
x
of
\mathbb{P}[A\leq x]
.

We first state in Sec.
III
our main results for the relevant case of i.i.d. and constant-composition ensembles over generic
DMC
s. Additional results are shown in Sec.
IV
for general channels with few additional assumptions on the ensemble or conditions on the statistical behavior of the error probability as
n\to\infty
. The proofs of our theorems are included in Sec.
V
, while most lemmas thereby used are proved in the Appendix.

III
Discrete Memoryless Channels

In this section, we introduce our main concentration results for
DMC
s.
Our first result states the convergence in probability of the error exponent
E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
to the TRC exponent
E_{\rm trc}(R)
. Since the exponent of the probability of error is not a sum of i.i.d. terms, the weak law of large numbers cannot be applied. This result holds for i.i.d. and constant-composition ensembles over
DMC
s with input distribution
Q
.

Theorem 1

For a general DMC channel, i.i.d. and constant-composition ensembles and rates
0\leq R<C
, it holds that

\displaystyle E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\rm p})}}{{\,\longrightarrow\,}}}E_{\rm trc}(R,Q).

(13)

Proof:

Sec.
V-A
.
∎

Theorem
1
not only proves the achievability of the TRC exponent, but also shows that the probability of finding a code in the ensemble with higher or lower exponent than the TRC exponent tends to zero. The above concentration property gives more information about the error exponent behaviour of the ensemble than the traditional derivation of the random coding error exponent, which computes the exponent of the expected error probability. This way, the TRC emerges as the most likely error exponent for pairwise-independent random-coding ensembles as the block length
n
tends to infinity —if one wishes to improve the error exponent, one must improve the ensemble. The
TRC
exponent is lower than or equal to the expurgated exponent and can in some case be strictly smaller. This implies that the codes in the pairwise independent ensemble that achieve the expurgated exponent are not typical codes and are unlikely to be found by random generation.

The proof of Theorem
1
requires different techniques for the rate regimes
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)=E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
and
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)>E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
. For the first regime, corresponding to high rates such that
R_{\rm{crit}}(R,Q)\leq R\leq C
, where
R_{\rm{crit}}(R,Q)
is the critical rate, we exploit the fact that
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)=E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
and the Levy’s continuity theorem
[
20
, Sec. XIII.1]
to obtain that the moment-generating function of the random variable
E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})
converges to the moment-generating function of a deterministic variable with value
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
. In other words,
E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})
converges in distribution to a constant
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
, implying the convergence in probability in (
13
) for both the i.i.d. and the constant-composition ensembles.

For the second regime where
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)>E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
, corresponding to low rates, we shift the convergence analysis to the union upper bound on the error probability
P_{\rm e}^{\rm ub}({\cal C}_{n})
in (
20
), a bound that is tight enough at low rates. Then, we use De Caen’s inequality
[
21
]
, a bound proved to be tight for the random-coding ensemble average error probability in
[
22
]
, to argue that such convergence also happens for the exact error probability
P_{\rm e}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
. In particular, we set
\delta=3\varepsilon
in (
11
) and write

\mathbb{P}\big[|E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})-E_{\rm trc}(R)|>3\varepsilon\big]\leq\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}+\gamma_{n},

(14)

where
\alpha_{n}
accounts for the convergence of the exact error exponent to that of the union bound

\alpha_{n}=\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})-E_{n}^{\rm ub}(\mathcal{C}_{n})\big|>\varepsilon\Big],

(15)

the second term studies the convergence of the error exponent of the union bound,
E_{n}^{\rm ub}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
to its ensemble average,

\beta_{n}=\mathbb{P}\bigg[\bigg|E_{n}^{\rm ub}(\mathcal{C}_{n})-\left(-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\log P_{\mathrm{e}}^{\rm ub}(\mathcal{C}_{n})\big]\right)\bigg|>\varepsilon\bigg],

(16)

and the last term deals with the convergence of such ensemble average to the TRC, namely

\gamma_{n}=\mathbb{P}\bigg[\bigg|\left(-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\log P_{\mathrm{e}}^{\rm ub}(\mathcal{C}_{n})\big]\right)-E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)\bigg|>\varepsilon\bigg].

(17)

For the i.i.d. ensemble, we exploit the symbol-wise independence to show that the three terms
\alpha_{n}
,
\beta_{n}
and
\gamma_{n}
vanish as
n\to\infty
in (
14
), hence obtaining our result in (
13
). For constant-composition codes, we are also able to obtain a vanishing
\alpha_{n}
,
\beta_{n}
and
\gamma_{n}
exploiting the independence of the joint type between two pairs of codewords and consider the expression of the limiting ensemble average of
E_{n}^{\rm ub}({\cal C}_{n})
for constant-composition codes reported in
[
9
]
.

Theorem
1
shows the converge of sequences of random variables to the statistical mean. A refined analysis to that of Theorem
1
consists of studying, separately, the probability tails involved in the definition of convergence in probability in (
11
). The work in
[
12
]
, addressed this issue for the constant-composition ensemble over
DMC
s. Specifically,
[
12
]
showed an interesting asymmetry: the probability
\mathbb{P}[E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})<E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)]
decays exponentially, while
\mathbb{P}[E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})>E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)]
decays double-exponentially. This implies that, beyond the concentration property, it is significantly more difficult to find a code in the ensemble with exponent higher than
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
.

We next derive some results on the convergence rate of the error exponent
E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})
to the typical random-coding exponent
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
.

Theorem 2

For the i.i.d. or constant-composition ensembles with rate
0\leq R\leq C
and any
\varepsilon>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})<E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)-\varepsilon\big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle.}}{{\leq}}2^{-n\varepsilon},

(18)

that is an exponential decay in the coding blocklength. In addition, for any
0<R\leq C
and
\varepsilon>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})>E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)+\varepsilon\big]=O\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg).

(19)

Proof:

Sec.
V-B
.
∎

Theorem
2
, that strengthens Theorem
1
, implicitly assumes that
E_{\rm trc}^{\rm ub}(R,Q)=E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)
for all rates below capacity to obtain (
18
) and uses the Berry-Esseen theorem
[
23
]
to obtain (
19
). For the union bound to the error probability,

\displaystyle P_{\mathrm{e}}^{\rm ub}(\mathscr{c}_{n})=\frac{1}{M_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{n}}\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}_{i}\to\bm{x}_{j}],

(20)

we are able to refine the upper tail in (
19
) as follows.

Theorem 3

For all rates satisfying
E_{\rm trc}(R,Q)>E_{\rm rce}(R,Q)
, there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that the following holds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[E_{n}^{\rm ub}({\cal C}_{n})\geq E_{\rm ex}(R)+\epsilon\right]\leq 2^{-2^{n\epsilon}},

(21)

where
E_{\rm ex}(R)
is the expurgated error exponent
[
24
]
.

Proof:

Appendix
V-C
.
∎

For strictly zero rate, that is
R=0
, the expurgated is tight. Therefore, we have the following result.

Theorem 4

For the i.i.d. or constant-composition ensembles with rate
R=0
and any
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})\geq E_{\rm trc}(0,Q)+\epsilon\right]\leq 2^{-2^{n\epsilon}}.

(22)

Proof:

Sec.
V-D
.
∎So far, we have introduced results related to the convergence in probability of the error exponent for pairwise-independent random codes. In the remaining of the section, we discuss the concentration in distribution of the error exponent
E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
to its ensemble-average
\mathbb{E}[E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})]
as
n\to\infty
at the low rate regime. While Theorem
5
, valid for an exactly constant number of messages, states that the random-coding error exponent converges to a Gaussian-like distribution, we let the number of messages
M_{n}
in Theorem
6
to grow sub-exponentially with
n
, yet at a minimum rate
M_{n}\gg\sqrt{n}
, and show that it converges to a Gaussian. As in Theorem
6
and Theorem
13
, most of the following results use the Stein’s method
[
25
]
.

Theorem 5

Let
M_{n}=M
be a constant number of messages, fixed for every
n
, and let
U_{ij}\sim{\cal N}(0,1)
, for
i=1,\ldots,M
and
j=1,\ldots,M
such that
i\neq j
, be a set of independent standard normal random variables. Then, the error exponent for both i.i.d. and constant-composition random-coding ensembles satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})-\mathbb{E}[E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})]}{\sqrt{\var(E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n}))}}\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\rm d})}}{{\,\longrightarrow\,}}}\frac{\min_{i\neq j}U_{ij}-\mathbb{E}[\min_{i\neq j}U_{ij}]}{\sqrt{\var(\min_{i\neq j}U_{ij})}}.

(23)

Proof:

Sec.
V-E
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
5
is based on the fact that
E_{n}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
is a minimization of a constant number of
M(M-1)
terms where each term is a sum of independent random variables in the i.i.d. ensemble, and a sum of dependent random variables with an additional vanishing term in the constant-composition ensemble. Hence, the central limit theorm
[
20
, Ch. VIII]
and the Levy’s continuity theorem
[
23
]
can be applied. In fact, it is easy to see that (
23
) holds when the exponent of the pairwise error probability of two different codewords
\bm{X}_{i}
and
\bm{X}_{j}
, that is
-\frac{1}{n}\log\mathbb{P}[\bm{X}_{i}\to\bm{X}_{j}]
forms a set of independent random variables. However, even for the i.i.d. ensemble, such variables are only pairwise-independent. To give an example, for the binary symmetric channel with only three codewords
\bm{X}_{1}
,
\bm{X}_{2}
and
\bm{X}_{3}
, given the Hamming distance between two pairs, the Hamming distance between the third pair is not independent on the previous ones. Theorem
5
argues that, despite such dependence, this becomes negligible as
n\to\infty
when the number of codewords is constant.

We illustrate in Fig.
1
the histogram of the error exponent
E_{n}({\cal C}_{n})
used over a binary symmetric channel (BSC) with bit-flipping probability
p=0.11
, equiprobable bits and
M=4
codewords for a blocklength of
n=10,000
. The histograms are obtained for the i.i.d. and constant-composition ensembles using the Monte Carlo method after
10^{7}
trials. For the sake of comparison, we also depict the asymptotic distribution of the random variable
\min_{i\neq j}U_{ij}
in the right-hand side of (
23
) (solid), and a normal approximation with the same mean and variance (dashed). We observe that the histogram matches the Gaussian-like distribution predicted by Theorem
5
, with a slightly asymmetric tail tilting.
